========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these times where I eat a lot more than I mean to. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should finally say it out loud to somebody.

It usually happens after really long days on site, or sometimes after a neighborhood meeting when I’m just kind of worn out and keyed up at the same time. I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.

It’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.

I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.

It’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. So then when I’m home and I can’t even manage something as basic as stopping eating when I meant to, it feels pretty awful.

But I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt or just being upset with myself. It’s definitely not a neutral feeling afterward.

I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. So then when I’m home and I can’t even manage something as basic as stopping eating when I meant to, it feels pretty awful.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt or just being upset with myself. It’s definitely not a neutral feeling afterward.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / first_line_supervisor_of_construction_trades_or_extraction_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Um… I don’t really know that I’ve noticed anything specific there. I mean, it’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, I guess. My focus has mostly been on the eating stuff and how I feel afterward, so when you ask about sex life, I kind of have to stop and think.

I think so, yes. I mean… when I’m actually present and not distracted in my own head, it can be enjoyable. I wouldn’t say that part is completely gone or anything.

But nothing jumps out to me as a big change in interest recently.\n\nDoctor: When you do have sex, do you find it enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I always think of it as “comforting myself” in the moment, because it feels more automatic than that, but when I look at when it happens, it’s usually after those really long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to be “on” the whole time.

I’ll get home and I’m just kind of exhausted and wound up at the same time, and then I start eating whatever is easiest. Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.

So yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these times where I eat a lot more than I mean to. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should finally say it out loud to somebody.\n\nIt usually happens after really long days on site, or sometimes after a neighborhood meeting when I’m just kind of worn out and keyed up at the same time. I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.

What made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. Then I start eating whatever is there — leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random things — and it turns into way more than I meant to eat. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.

So, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these times where I eat a lot more than I mean to. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should finally say it out loud to somebody.\n\nIt usually happens after really long days on site, or sometimes after a neighborhood meeting when I’m just kind of worn out and keyed up at the same time. I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.

I’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.

I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. So then when I’m home and I can’t even manage something as basic as stopping eating when I meant to, it feels pretty awful.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt or just being upset with myself. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I mean, I can see how it might be that, because when I’m stressed or upset it does seem to happen more, and when I’m eating I’m not really thinking as much about whatever happened that day.

But I don’t know if I would say I’m consciously trying to “escape” my emotions. In the moment it feels more like I’m on autopilot. Like I get home, I’m tired, I’m tense, and I start eating because it’s there and it gives me something to do. Then somewhere in the middle of it, I realize I’m not really paying attention anymore — not to the food, not to how full I am, not to anything really.

So… maybe yes, in a way, but I don’t think I fully understand it while it’s happening. Looking back, it does seem like it gives me a break from feeling wound up or upset, even if afterward I don’t feel better about it.

It’s definitely not a neutral feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’ve noticed anything specific there. I mean, it’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, I guess. My focus has mostly been on the eating stuff and how I feel afterward, so when you ask about sex life, I kind of have to stop and think.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s some clear new problem I came in worried about, though. Nothing jumps out to me as, like, “this has changed recently and I need help with it.” If you mean interest, or performance, or relationship stuff, I might need you to be a little more specific, but off the top of my head… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed a clear decrease. It’s a little hard for me to separate out, because I haven’t really been paying close attention to it in that way. Like, I’m not sitting there comparing this month to last month or anything.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I suddenly don’t want sex at all, then no, that doesn’t really sound right. I wouldn’t say there’s been some obvious drop that I’ve been worried about. It’s more that other stuff has been taking up a lot of mental space, so I may not be as tuned into that part of my life as I normally would be. But nothing jumps out to me as a big change in interest recently.\n\nDoctor: When you do have sex, do you find it enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… when I’m actually present and not distracted in my own head, it can be enjoyable. I wouldn’t say that part is completely gone or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m a little in my head, like thinking too much or noticing how I feel about myself, but I don’t know that I’d describe sex itself as unenjoyable. It’s more variable, I guess. If I’m relaxed, then yes, I can enjoy it. If I’m stressed or preoccupied, then it’s harder to really be in it, but I think that’s probably true for a lot of people. So… overall, yes, I still can enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to comfort yourself when you're feeling stressed or upset after those long days or meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I always think of it as “comforting myself” in the moment, because it feels more automatic than that, but when I look at when it happens, it’s usually after those really long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to be “on” the whole time.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m just kind of exhausted and wound up at the same time, and then I start eating whatever is easiest. Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.\n\nSo yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like anxious or upset, rather than just physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.\n\nWhat made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating is a way for you to deal with or escape from your emotions when you're feeling stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. Yeah… I think I do, at least sometimes. What made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after one of those neighborhood meetings where I’m still replaying everything in my head, I’ll come home and eat way more than I meant to.

It usually doesn’t start out like I’m planning to do that. It’ll be something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then maybe more of whatever’s there. And by the time I really notice what I’m doing, I’m uncomfortably full and kind of embarrassed, like, why didn’t I just stop earlier?

So I guess, yes, it does seem like a way I cope when I’m stressed or wound up, even if I’m not thinking of it that way in the moment.

I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. So then when I’m home and I can’t even manage something as basic as stopping eating when I meant to, it feels pretty awful.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt or just being upset with myself. It’s definitely not a neutral feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’ve noticed anything specific there. I mean, it’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, I guess. My focus has mostly been on the eating stuff and how I feel afterward, so when you ask about sex life, I kind of have to stop and think.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s some clear new problem I came in worried about, though. Nothing jumps out to me as, like, “this has changed recently and I need help with it.” If you mean interest, or performance, or relationship stuff, I might need you to be a little more specific, but off the top of my head… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed a clear decrease. It’s a little hard for me to separate out, because I haven’t really been paying close attention to it in that way. Like, I’m not sitting there comparing this month to last month or anything.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I suddenly don’t want sex at all, then no, that doesn’t really sound right. I wouldn’t say there’s been some obvious drop that I’ve been worried about. It’s more that other stuff has been taking up a lot of mental space, so I may not be as tuned into that part of my life as I normally would be. But nothing jumps out to me as a big change in interest recently.\n\nDoctor: When you do have sex, do you find it enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… when I’m actually present and not distracted in my own head, it can be enjoyable. I wouldn’t say that part is completely gone or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m a little in my head, like thinking too much or noticing how I feel about myself, but I don’t know that I’d describe sex itself as unenjoyable. It’s more variable, I guess. If I’m relaxed, then yes, I can enjoy it. If I’m stressed or preoccupied, then it’s harder to really be in it, but I think that’s probably true for a lot of people. So… overall, yes, I still can enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to comfort yourself when you're feeling stressed or upset after those long days or meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I always think of it as “comforting myself” in the moment, because it feels more automatic than that, but when I look at when it happens, it’s usually after those really long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to be “on” the whole time.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m just kind of exhausted and wound up at the same time, and then I start eating whatever is easiest. Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.\n\nSo yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like anxious or upset, rather than just physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.\n\nWhat made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. Then I start eating whatever is there — leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random things — and it turns into way more than I meant to eat. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating is a way for you to deal with or escape from your emotions when you're feeling stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I mean, I can see how it might be that, because when I’m stressed or upset it does seem to happen more, and when I’m eating I’m not really thinking as much about whatever happened that day.\n\nBut I don’t know if I would say I’m consciously trying to “escape” my emotions. In the moment it feels more like I’m on autopilot. Like I get home, I’m tired, I’m tense, and I start eating because it’s there and it gives me something to do. Then somewhere in the middle of it, I realize I’m not really paying attention anymore — not to the food, not to how full I am, not to anything really.\n\nSo… maybe yes, in a way, but I don’t think I fully understand it while it’s happening. Looking back, it does seem like it gives me a break from feeling wound up or upset, even if afterward I don’t feel better about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior and your sex life are related in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as connected.\n\nI mean… my eating definitely seems tied to stress and feeling overwhelmed, like we were talking about, but when you ask about my sex life specifically, I don’t immediately have an answer like, “Yes, this happens because of that.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean — like whether eating affects how I feel about my body, or whether relationship stuff or intimacy affects the eating?\n\nI don’t have a clear pattern I’ve noticed. It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head. So I don’t want to say no in a way that sounds definite, because maybe there’s something there I haven’t paid attention to, but right now… I can’t really point to a connection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least sometimes. What made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after one of those neighborhood meetings where I’m still replaying everything in my head, I’ll come home and eat way more than I meant to.\n\nIt usually doesn’t start out like I’m planning to do that. It’ll be something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then maybe more of whatever’s there. And by the time I really notice what I’m doing, I’m uncomfortably full and kind of embarrassed, like, why didn’t I just stop earlier?\n\nSo I guess, yes, it does seem like a way I cope when I’m stressed or wound up, even if I’m not thinking of it that way in the moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

So I don’t want to say no in a way that sounds definite, because maybe there’s something there I haven’t paid attention to, but right now… I can’t really point to a connection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least sometimes. What made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after one of those neighborhood meetings where I’m still replaying everything in my head, I’ll come home and eat way more than I meant to.\n\nIt usually doesn’t start out like I’m planning to do that. It’ll be something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then maybe more of whatever’s there. And by the time I really notice what I’m doing, I’m uncomfortably full and kind of embarrassed, like, why didn’t I just stop earlier?\n\nSo I guess, yes, it does seem like a way I cope when I’m stressed or wound up, even if I’m not thinking of it that way in the moment.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your eating is directly triggered by your emotions, such as feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / first_line_supervisor_of_construction_trades_or_extraction_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Eating_Large_Amount\nmanifestation: \"What made me bring it up is that after some long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, I’ll come home and eat way more than I meant to—leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever’s there—until I’m uncomfortably full.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. Not every single time, but there’s definitely a pattern where if I’ve had a really long day on site, or if there was a neighborhood meeting that left me tense or frustrated, I’ll come home and it’s like I just start eating way more than I meant to.

It might start with something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then just whatever’s there. It’s not like I sit down thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat all this.” It’s more like I’m already keyed up, and once I start, I kind of keep going until I’m uncomfortably full. Then afterward I realize how much it was.

So, yes, stress or feeling wound up does seem to trigger it, even if I don’t always recognize that until after.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these times where I eat a lot more than I mean to. I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. So then when I’m home and I can’t even manage something as basic as stopping eating when I meant to, it feels pretty awful.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt or just being upset with myself. It’s definitely not a neutral feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’ve noticed anything specific there. I mean, it’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, I guess. My focus has mostly been on the eating stuff and how I feel afterward, so when you ask about sex life, I kind of have to stop and think.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s some clear new problem I came in worried about, though. Nothing jumps out to me as, like, “this has changed recently and I need help with it.” If you mean interest, or performance, or relationship stuff, I might need you to be a little more specific, but off the top of my head… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed a clear decrease. Like, I’m not sitting there comparing this month to last month or anything.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I suddenly don’t want sex at all, then no, that doesn’t really sound right. I wouldn’t say there’s been some obvious drop that I’ve been worried about. It’s more that other stuff has been taking up a lot of mental space, so I may not be as tuned into that part of my life as I normally would be. But nothing jumps out to me as a big change in interest recently.\n\nDoctor: When you do have sex, do you find it enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… when I’m actually present and not distracted in my own head, it can be enjoyable. I wouldn’t say that part is completely gone or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m a little in my head, like thinking too much or noticing how I feel about myself, but I don’t know that I’d describe sex itself as unenjoyable. It’s more variable, I guess. If I’m relaxed, then yes, I can enjoy it. If I’m stressed or preoccupied, then it’s harder to really be in it, but I think that’s probably true for a lot of people. So… overall, yes, I still can enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to comfort yourself when you're feeling stressed or upset after those long days or meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I always think of it as “comforting myself” in the moment, because it feels more automatic than that, but when I look at when it happens, it’s usually after those really long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to be “on” the whole time.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m just kind of exhausted and wound up at the same time, and then I start eating whatever is easiest. Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.\n\nSo yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like anxious or upset, rather than just physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.\n\nWhat made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. Then I start eating whatever is there — leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random things — and it turns into way more than I meant to eat. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating is a way for you to deal with or escape from your emotions when you're feeling stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I mean, I can see how it might be that, because when I’m stressed or upset it does seem to happen more, and when I’m eating I’m not really thinking as much about whatever happened that day.\n\nBut I don’t know if I would say I’m consciously trying to “escape” my emotions. In the moment it feels more like I’m on autopilot. Like I get home, I’m tired, I’m tense, and I start eating because it’s there and it gives me something to do. Then somewhere in the middle of it, I realize I’m not really paying attention anymore — not to the food, not to how full I am, not to anything really.\n\nSo… maybe yes, in a way, but I don’t think I fully understand it while it’s happening. Looking back, it does seem like it gives me a break from feeling wound up or upset, even if afterward I don’t feel better about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior and your sex life are related in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as connected.\n\nI mean… my eating definitely seems tied to stress and feeling overwhelmed, like we were talking about, but when you ask about my sex life specifically, I don’t immediately have an answer like, “Yes, this happens because of that.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean — like whether eating affects how I feel about my body, or whether relationship stuff or intimacy affects the eating?\n\nI don’t have a clear pattern I’ve noticed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I mean, I can see how they could be related in a general way, like if I’m feeling bad about my body after eating too much, maybe I wouldn’t feel especially interested in being close to someone. But I haven’t actually noticed a clear cause-and-effect thing, like “I ate this way, and then I didn’t want sex,” or the other way around.

It’s a little hard for me to separate it out, honestly, because I don’t always think about sex as its own separate issue. Sometimes I’m just tired or stressed or kind of in my head, and eating is part of that same messy pile of things. But I can’t say for sure that my eating behavior is causing a lack of interest in sex.

So… maybe there’s some connection, but I haven’t really tracked it or understood it that way.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F52.7\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these times where I eat a lot more than I mean to. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should finally say it out loud to somebody.\n\nIt usually happens after really long days on site, or sometimes after a neighborhood meeting when I’m just kind of worn out and keyed up at the same time. I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food. Like I’ll finish something and think, that should be it, and then a few minutes later I’m back in the kitchen again. So… yeah, I guess I’d say it feels like I can’t really stop once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I definitely don’t feel good afterward. I’ll sit there and kind of replay it in my head, like, “Why did I let that happen again?” But whether I’d call it guilt exactly… I don’t know.\n\nIt’s more like embarrassment and frustration with myself, I guess. Sometimes I feel kind of stupid for it, like I should have more control, especially because at work I’m used to being the person who keeps things organized and moving. So then when I’m home and I can’t even manage something as basic as stopping eating when I meant to, it feels pretty awful.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as guilt or just being upset with myself. It’s definitely not a neutral feeling afterward.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any issues with your sex life recently?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know that I’ve noticed anything specific there. I mean, it’s not something I’ve been tracking closely, I guess. My focus has mostly been on the eating stuff and how I feel afterward, so when you ask about sex life, I kind of have to stop and think.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s some clear new problem I came in worried about, though. Nothing jumps out to me as, like, “this has changed recently and I need help with it.” If you mean interest, or performance, or relationship stuff, I might need you to be a little more specific, but off the top of my head… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed a clear decrease. It’s a little hard for me to separate out, because I haven’t really been paying close attention to it in that way. Like, I’m not sitting there comparing this month to last month or anything.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I suddenly don’t want sex at all, then no, that doesn’t really sound right. I wouldn’t say there’s been some obvious drop that I’ve been worried about. It’s more that other stuff has been taking up a lot of mental space, so I may not be as tuned into that part of my life as I normally would be. But nothing jumps out to me as a big change in interest recently.\n\nDoctor: When you do have sex, do you find it enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… when I’m actually present and not distracted in my own head, it can be enjoyable. I wouldn’t say that part is completely gone or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m a little in my head, like thinking too much or noticing how I feel about myself, but I don’t know that I’d describe sex itself as unenjoyable. It’s more variable, I guess. If I’m relaxed, then yes, I can enjoy it. If I’m stressed or preoccupied, then it’s harder to really be in it, but I think that’s probably true for a lot of people. So… overall, yes, I still can enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to comfort yourself when you're feeling stressed or upset after those long days or meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I always think of it as “comforting myself” in the moment, because it feels more automatic than that, but when I look at when it happens, it’s usually after those really long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to be “on” the whole time.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m just kind of exhausted and wound up at the same time, and then I start eating whatever is easiest. Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.\n\nSo yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like anxious or upset, rather than just physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.\n\nWhat made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. Then I start eating whatever is there — leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random things — and it turns into way more than I meant to eat. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating is a way for you to deal with or escape from your emotions when you're feeling stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I mean, I can see how it might be that, because when I’m stressed or upset it does seem to happen more, and when I’m eating I’m not really thinking as much about whatever happened that day.\n\nBut I don’t know if I would say I’m consciously trying to “escape” my emotions. In the moment it feels more like I’m on autopilot. Like I get home, I’m tired, I’m tense, and I start eating because it’s there and it gives me something to do. Then somewhere in the middle of it, I realize I’m not really paying attention anymore — not to the food, not to how full I am, not to anything really.\n\nSo… maybe yes, in a way, but I don’t think I fully understand it while it’s happening. Looking back, it does seem like it gives me a break from feeling wound up or upset, even if afterward I don’t feel better about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior and your sex life are related in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as connected.\n\nI mean… my eating definitely seems tied to stress and feeling overwhelmed, like we were talking about, but when you ask about my sex life specifically, I don’t immediately have an answer like, “Yes, this happens because of that.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean — like whether eating affects how I feel about my body, or whether relationship stuff or intimacy affects the eating?\n\nI don’t have a clear pattern I’ve noticed. It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head. So I don’t want to say no in a way that sounds definite, because maybe there’s something there I haven’t paid attention to, but right now… I can’t really point to a connection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least sometimes. What made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after one of those neighborhood meetings where I’m still replaying everything in my head, I’ll come home and eat way more than I meant to.\n\nIt usually doesn’t start out like I’m planning to do that. It’ll be something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then maybe more of whatever’s there. And by the time I really notice what I’m doing, I’m uncomfortably full and kind of embarrassed, like, why didn’t I just stop earlier?\n\nSo I guess, yes, it does seem like a way I cope when I’m stressed or wound up, even if I’m not thinking of it that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is directly triggered by your emotions, such as feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not every single time, but there’s definitely a pattern where if I’ve had a really long day on site, or if there was a neighborhood meeting that left me tense or frustrated, I’ll come home and it’s like I just start eating way more than I meant to.\n\nIt might start with something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then just whatever’s there. It’s not like I sit down thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat all this.” It’s more like I’m already keyed up, and once I start, I kind of keep going until I’m uncomfortably full. Then afterward I realize how much it was.\n\nSo, yes, stress or feeling wound up does seem to trigger it, even if I don’t always recognize that until after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior is related to your lack of interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I can see how they could be related in a general way, like if I’m feeling bad about my body after eating too much, maybe I wouldn’t feel especially interested in being close to someone. But I haven’t actually noticed a clear cause-and-effect thing, like “I ate this way, and then I didn’t want sex,” or the other way around.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to separate it out, honestly, because I don’t always think about sex as its own separate issue. Sometimes I’m just tired or stressed or kind of in my head, and eating is part of that same messy pile of things. But I can’t say for sure that my eating behavior is causing a lack of interest in sex.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some connection, but I haven’t really tracked it or understood it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Nothing jumps out to me as, like, “this has changed recently and I need help with it.” If you mean interest, or performance, or relationship stuff, I might need you to be a little more specific, but off the top of my head… no, not really.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your interest in sex recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t noticed a clear decrease. It’s a little hard for me to separate out, because I haven’t really been paying close attention to it in that way. Like, I’m not sitting there comparing this month to last month or anything.\n\nIf you’re asking whether I suddenly don’t want sex at all, then no, that doesn’t really sound right. I wouldn’t say there’s been some obvious drop that I’ve been worried about. It’s more that other stuff has been taking up a lot of mental space, so I may not be as tuned into that part of my life as I normally would be. But nothing jumps out to me as a big change in interest recently.\n\nDoctor: When you do have sex, do you find it enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. I mean… when I’m actually present and not distracted in my own head, it can be enjoyable. I wouldn’t say that part is completely gone or anything.\n\nSometimes I’m a little in my head, like thinking too much or noticing how I feel about myself, but I don’t know that I’d describe sex itself as unenjoyable. It’s more variable, I guess. If I’m relaxed, then yes, I can enjoy it. If I’m stressed or preoccupied, then it’s harder to really be in it, but I think that’s probably true for a lot of people. So… overall, yes, I still can enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating happens because you're trying to comfort yourself when you're feeling stressed or upset after those long days or meetings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it. I don’t know if I always think of it as “comforting myself” in the moment, because it feels more automatic than that, but when I look at when it happens, it’s usually after those really long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to be “on” the whole time.\n\nI’ll get home and I’m just kind of exhausted and wound up at the same time, and then I start eating whatever is easiest. Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.\n\nSo yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like anxious or upset, rather than just physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.\n\nWhat made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. Then I start eating whatever is there — leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random things — and it turns into way more than I meant to eat. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating is a way for you to deal with or escape from your emotions when you're feeling stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I mean, I can see how it might be that, because when I’m stressed or upset it does seem to happen more, and when I’m eating I’m not really thinking as much about whatever happened that day.\n\nBut I don’t know if I would say I’m consciously trying to “escape” my emotions. In the moment it feels more like I’m on autopilot. Like I get home, I’m tired, I’m tense, and I start eating because it’s there and it gives me something to do. Then somewhere in the middle of it, I realize I’m not really paying attention anymore — not to the food, not to how full I am, not to anything really.\n\nSo… maybe yes, in a way, but I don’t think I fully understand it while it’s happening. Looking back, it does seem like it gives me a break from feeling wound up or upset, even if afterward I don’t feel better about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior and your sex life are related in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as connected.\n\nI mean… my eating definitely seems tied to stress and feeling overwhelmed, like we were talking about, but when you ask about my sex life specifically, I don’t immediately have an answer like, “Yes, this happens because of that.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean — like whether eating affects how I feel about my body, or whether relationship stuff or intimacy affects the eating?\n\nI don’t have a clear pattern I’ve noticed. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it probably does, at least indirectly. It’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “I feel bad about my body, so I don’t want sex,” but there are definitely times where after I’ve eaten too much, I feel really aware of my stomach and how my clothes fit, and then the idea of being seen or touched just feels… harder.

Honestly, it’s been worse since I started wearing more fitted clothes at work. In construction settings I used to dress more practically and kind of hide in looser stuff, but now, being in front of people more at meetings, I catch myself judging the whole day by whether my pants feel tight or whether I think people can see my stomach. Like if I’m sitting in a meeting and I feel the waistband pressing in, it can completely change how I feel about myself for the rest of the day.

So if I already have that running in my head, and then I come home feeling full or bloated, I’m not exactly in a place where I feel attractive or relaxed. I get self-conscious, and I think that probably makes me pull back from wanting sex or even wanting that kind of closeness. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds kind of shallow when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really consuming.

Like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, whatever is around. And it’s not like I planned to eat that much — it starts as just grabbing something, and then it keeps going until I’m way more full than I meant to be, like uncomfortably full.\n\nSo yes, I think stress or being upset is tied into it. It’s almost like once I’m home and nobody needs anything from me for a second, I just sort of let go, but in a way that doesn’t feel good afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you eat more when you're feeling emotional, like anxious or upset, rather than just physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s more accurate than saying I’m just hungry. Like, there are definitely times I’m physically hungry after work, especially if I’ve been running around all day, but the eating I’m talking about feels different.\n\nWhat made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after a neighborhood meeting where I’ve had to deal with people and stay composed, I’ll come home and it’s like I’m still carrying all of that stress in my body. Then I start eating whatever is there — leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random things — and it turns into way more than I meant to eat. It doesn’t feel like, “Oh, I’m hungry and I’m making dinner.” It’s more like I’m anxious or upset or just wrung out, and I keep going until I’m uncomfortably full.\n\nSo, yes, I do think the emotional part is a big piece of it. It’s not always that I can name the feeling right away, but looking back, it’s usually not just regular hunger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating helps you calm down or feel better when you're stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, at least at first. It’s not like I sit there thinking, “This will calm me down,” exactly, but after those long days on site or after a neighborhood meeting, when I get home and I’m still all keyed up, eating is sort of the easiest thing to fall into.\n\nI’ll start with something that feels normal, like leftover takeout or a bowl of cereal, but then it just keeps going — chips, more cereal, whatever is there — and for a little while it kind of quiets everything down. Like I don’t have to think about the day or replay conversations in my head. But then I end up eating way more than I meant to, to the point that I’m uncomfortably full, and then it doesn’t really feel calming anymore. It feels like, “Why did I do that again?” So it helps in the moment, maybe, but not for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating is a way for you to deal with or escape from your emotions when you're feeling stressed or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to separate those things, honestly. I mean, I can see how it might be that, because when I’m stressed or upset it does seem to happen more, and when I’m eating I’m not really thinking as much about whatever happened that day.\n\nBut I don’t know if I would say I’m consciously trying to “escape” my emotions. In the moment it feels more like I’m on autopilot. Like I get home, I’m tired, I’m tense, and I start eating because it’s there and it gives me something to do. Then somewhere in the middle of it, I realize I’m not really paying attention anymore — not to the food, not to how full I am, not to anything really.\n\nSo… maybe yes, in a way, but I don’t think I fully understand it while it’s happening. Looking back, it does seem like it gives me a break from feeling wound up or upset, even if afterward I don’t feel better about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior and your sex life are related in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really thought about those two things as connected.\n\nI mean… my eating definitely seems tied to stress and feeling overwhelmed, like we were talking about, but when you ask about my sex life specifically, I don’t immediately have an answer like, “Yes, this happens because of that.” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean — like whether eating affects how I feel about my body, or whether relationship stuff or intimacy affects the eating?\n\nI don’t have a clear pattern I’ve noticed. It’s not something I’ve been tracking in my head. So I don’t want to say no in a way that sounds definite, because maybe there’s something there I haven’t paid attention to, but right now… I can’t really point to a connection.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you use eating as a way to cope with your emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, at least sometimes. What made me bring it up is that after some really long days on site, or after one of those neighborhood meetings where I’m still replaying everything in my head, I’ll come home and eat way more than I meant to.\n\nIt usually doesn’t start out like I’m planning to do that. It’ll be something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then maybe more of whatever’s there. And by the time I really notice what I’m doing, I’m uncomfortably full and kind of embarrassed, like, why didn’t I just stop earlier?\n\nSo I guess, yes, it does seem like a way I cope when I’m stressed or wound up, even if I’m not thinking of it that way in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating is directly triggered by your emotions, such as feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Not every single time, but there’s definitely a pattern where if I’ve had a really long day on site, or if there was a neighborhood meeting that left me tense or frustrated, I’ll come home and it’s like I just start eating way more than I meant to.\n\nIt might start with something normal, like leftover takeout, and then I’ll have cereal, and then chips, and then just whatever’s there. It’s not like I sit down thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat all this.” It’s more like I’m already keyed up, and once I start, I kind of keep going until I’m uncomfortably full. Then afterward I realize how much it was.\n\nSo, yes, stress or feeling wound up does seem to trigger it, even if I don’t always recognize that until after.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating behavior is related to your lack of interest in sex?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I can see how they could be related in a general way, like if I’m feeling bad about my body after eating too much, maybe I wouldn’t feel especially interested in being close to someone. But I haven’t actually noticed a clear cause-and-effect thing, like “I ate this way, and then I didn’t want sex,” or the other way around.\n\nIt’s a little hard for me to separate it out, honestly, because I don’t always think about sex as its own separate issue. Sometimes I’m just tired or stressed or kind of in my head, and eating is part of that same messy pile of things. But I can’t say for sure that my eating behavior is causing a lack of interest in sex.\n\nSo… maybe there’s some connection, but I haven’t really tracked it or understood it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think feeling bad about your body after eating too much affects your interest in sex?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably does, at least indirectly. It’s not always like I’m consciously thinking, “I feel bad about my body, so I don’t want sex,” but there are definitely times where after I’ve eaten too much, I feel really aware of my stomach and how my clothes fit, and then the idea of being seen or touched just feels… harder.\n\nHonestly, it’s been worse since I started wearing more fitted clothes at work. In construction settings I used to dress more practically and kind of hide in looser stuff, but now, being in front of people more at meetings, I catch myself judging the whole day by whether my pants feel tight or whether I think people can see my stomach. Like if I’m sitting in a meeting and I feel the waistband pressing in, it can completely change how I feel about myself for the rest of the day.\n\nSo if I already have that running in my head, and then I come home feeling full or bloated, I’m not exactly in a place where I feel attractive or relaxed. I get self-conscious, and I think that probably makes me pull back from wanting sex or even wanting that kind of closeness. It’s embarrassing to say, because it sounds kind of shallow when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really consuming.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think the eating is a problem, but I don’t know if it’s the whole problem by itself. It feels connected to this bigger thing about how I see my body, if that makes sense.

Like, since I started wearing more fitted clothes for work and being more up front in meetings, I notice I’m constantly checking how I feel in my clothes. I’ll judge the whole day by whether my pants feel tight, or whether I think people can see my stomach when I’m standing there talking. And then if I eat too much, it sort of confirms whatever bad feeling I already had about myself.

So the eating is definitely part of what’s bothering me, but I think it’s tied up with feeling really self-conscious about my weight and shape, especially at work. It’s like the food is the thing I notice most because it happens in a concrete way, but underneath it there’s this constant measuring myself and feeling uncomfortable in my own body.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having these times where I eat a lot more than I mean to. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should finally say it out loud to somebody.\n\nIt usually happens after really long days on site, or sometimes after a neighborhood meeting when I’m just kind of worn out and keyed up at the same time. I’ll get home and start eating whatever’s around—like leftover takeout, cereal, chips, just random stuff from the kitchen—and it turns into way more than I intended. I don’t even always feel like I’m making a real meal, it’s more like I’m just going from one thing to the next, and then suddenly I’m uncomfortably full and thinking, “Why did I do that?” So… that’s what made me make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you're eating more than you mean to, do you feel like you can stop yourself or do you feel like you can't stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… once it starts, it really doesn’t feel like I’m making normal choices anymore. It’s like I go on autopilot. I’ll be standing in the kitchen thinking, okay, stop now, you have to be up early, the crew starts early, you’re going to feel awful if you keep doing this… and then I’ll still open another cabinet or go back to the fridge.\n\nIt’s not like I don’t know I should stop. I’m very aware of it, actually, which makes it worse. I’m kind of arguing with myself the whole time, but my body just keeps moving back for more food.
